over 13.44 days. The limited
K2
data are in accord with the CSS period
of
P_{1}
=0.40349 d.

EPIC 229228175
(C7) = vs2f266 (Cseresnjes 2001) is in the Sagittarius dwarf galaxy. The
K2
photometry suffers from crowding by a nearby relatively bright star located
to the East of this star (see
Everest
image panels), which probably explains
why the mean magnitude from
Gaia
(DR2),
G
=18.531 mag (range
17.6-19.2), is significantly brighter than that derived from the
K2

fluxes,
Kp
=18.86 here, and
Kp
=19.18 from Molnár
et al.

(2018). The
K2
data acquired after BJD 2457353, and between
2457323.5 and 2457324.3, were found to be unreliable and were not used.
Also, the
Kp
magnitude range is unusually low. Despite these problems
the period and amplitude ratios derived from the
K2
data agree well with
Cseresjnes’ values derived from photographic plates.

EPIC 229228184
(C7) = vs1f243 (Cseresnjes 2001) is in the Sagittarius dwarf
galaxy. The periods and amplitudes derived from the
K2

photometry are in accord with (and more accurate than) the values derived by
Cseresjnes from photographic plates. Two close bright stars appear to have
caused the detrending to be poor, the considerable amplitude variation seen in
the PDCsap data (which probably is instrumental), and may explain why the
Gaia
(DR2) mean magnitude,
G
=18.162 mag (range 17.4-18.8 mag), is
considerably brighter than that derived from the
K2
fluxes,
Kp
=18.79 mag, but is consistent with the MAST value,
Kp
(MAST)=18.10 mag.
Similar problems exist for EPIC 229228194.

EPIC 229228194
(C7) = vs1f242 in the Sagittarius dwarf
galaxy (Cseresnjes 2001) - The RRd nature of this star was discovered by
Cseresjnes. The periods derived from the
K2
photometry (PDCsap data) are
in accord with those given by Cseresjnes from
B
photometry. Owing to the
star’s faintness,
<
Kp
>
=19.3 mag, and probable contamination by
neighbouring stars, the
A_{1}
and
A_{0}
amplitudes given in Table 3 probably
are too large.

EPIC 229228220
(C7) = vs3f170 in the Sagittarius dwarf
galaxy (Cseresnjes 2001) - The mean
Kp
magnitude is uncertain: Huber
et al.
(2016) give 19.20 mag, whereas the
PDCsap
fluxes transformed
with
Kp
=25.3-2.5\log(F)
give 19.97 mag. Thus the
PDCsap

amplitudes are uncertain. The
Pyke
data were normalized to 19.20 mag,
the mean magnitude given in the EPIC catalog, and the
Pyke
residuals
were found to be significantly lower than the
PDCsap
residuals, 32
mmag
vs.
89 mmag.

EPIC 229228811
(C8) - This star previously was observed by
the Catalina Sky Survey (302 CSS and 172 MLS epochs), where the mean
V

magnitudes from the CSS and MLS data sets differ by 0.17 mag. The mean
magnitudes derived from the
K2
fluxes, Kp=18.14 mag derived here and
Kp=18.651 derived by Molnár
et al.
(2018), are both fainter than
Kp(MAST)=17.700 mag; these man magnitudes are to be compared with that from
the
Gaia
(DR2) survey, G=18.050 mag (range 17.4-18.6). The dominant
periods derived from the CSS and
K2
photometry are in good agreement,
0.372905 d (CSS) versus 0.372910 d (
K2
).

EPIC 235631055
(C11) =
OGLE-BLG-RRLYR-17146
- With

P_{1}
=0.43278 d this is one of the longest period ‘classical’ RRd stars known,
hence [Fe/H] probably is very low.

EPIC 235794591
(C11) =
OGLE-BLG-RRLYR-00727
-
Long-period ‘classical’ RRd star, probably quite metal poor.

EPIC 236212613
(C11) =
OGLE-BLG-RRLYR-16881
- The radial periods were derived by analyzing the
combined
OGLE-IV
and
K2
photometry.

EPIC 245974758
(C
12
, C
19
) - For all
three C12 RRd stars the
EAP
data corrected with the
K2SC

algorithm were found to be superior to basic
EAP
photometry. The mean
magnitudes derived here from the
K2
fluxes, Kp=17.13 mag, and that derived by Molnár
et al.
(2018), 17.281 mag, are in agreement.
A cross-match of the EPIC coordinates with those in the
Gaia
DR2 and DR3 catalogues
did not result in a match (contrary to the match with the DR2 Id 2413839863087928064 by Molnár et al. 2018).
Considerably fainter is the mean magnitude given by the EPIC catalog,
Kp(MAST)=17.681 mag.

EPIC 246058914
(C
12
, C
19
) - The mean
magnitudes derived from the
K2
fluxes (Kp=17.02 mag derived here and
17.105 mag derived by Molnár
et al.
2018), by the EPIC catalog,
Kp(MAST)=16.891 mag, and from the
Gaia
(DR2) survey, G=17.044 mag
(range 16.2-17.6), are all in agreement. In the Petersen diagram this star
lies along the smooth curve defined by the ‘classical’ RRd stars. However,
both the
K2
and Catalina Sky Survey data both show the fundamental mode
to be more powerful than the first-overtone mode:
A_{0}/A_{1}
=1.04 (
K2

data), which is unusual for a classical RRd star. Many significant
combination frequencies were identified in a
SigSpec
/
Combine

analysis of the short-cadence data (
PyKE
pipeline).

EPIC 247334376
(C13) - This long-period, probable
low-metallicity RRd stars stands out for having the second largest amplitude
ratio,
A_{1}
/
A_{0}
=5.7 (caused primarily by the low value of
A_{0}=26.8\pm 1.4

mmag), resulting in the light curve showing little amplitude variation.

EPIC 248369176
(C10) - This star is one of the faintest in
the
K2
RRd sample. The mean magnitudes derived from the
K2
fluxes,
Kp=20.22 mag derived here and Kp=20.952 derived by Molnár
et al.
(2018),
are both faint and roughly consistent with G=20.317 mag (range 19.0-22.5) from
the
Gaia
(DR2) survey. The period
P_{1}
derived from the
K2

photometry is in agreement with the (sole) period derived from the Catalina Sky
Survey (CSS) data. The long periods suggest that the metallicity is very low.
Unfortunately the two available SDSS spectra have low S/N ratios and cannot
confirm the suspected low [Fe/H] (see Table 7 for
SDSS/SEGUE
physical
characteristics). A time series analysis found very little variability in the
Fourier
A_{1}
and
A_{0}
amplitudes.

EPIC 248426222
(C14) -
See Table 7 for
SDSS/SEGUE
physical characteristics.

EPIC 248509474
(C
14
) =
LINEAR
22657637 - In the Petersen diagram this long-period RRd star has a relatively small
P_{1}
/
P_{0}
ratio, but not so
low as to claim that it is an aRRd star.

EPIC 248514834
(C
14
) =
LINEAR
22316675 - This is the longest period
RRd star in the
K2
sample and therefore probably is very metal weak. It was
first identified as an RRd star by Poleski (2014) in his analysis of the

LINEAR
data.

EPIC 248653582
(
C14
) =
LINEAR
23135759 -
A time variability analysis suggests that there is no evidence for amplitude or
phase variability.

EPIC 248667792
(C14) - The
K2
photometry confirms (and
improves upon) the dominant period derived from the CSS data. Also identified
is the fundamental mode period.

EPIC 248845745
(C14) -
SDSS spectroscopy (see Table 7) via the
SEGUE pipeline shows [Fe/H]=–1.60
\pm
0.01 dex.

EPIC 248871792
(C
14
) =
LINEAR
23184879
- No evidence is seen for amplitude or phase modulation of the radial modes.
With
SigSpec
a total of 497 frequencies more significant than

sig
=
5 were identified in the SC data, almost all of which are
combination frequencies. See Table 7 for physical characteristics derived by
the
SDSS/SEGUE
pipeline.

EPIC 249790928
(C15) - This
Kp
=14.58 mag star is one of the
brighter RRd stars in the
K2
sample. The pulsation periods are long,
suggesting low metallicity. The (dominant) first-overtone mode exhibits weak
evidence of non-stationary modulation with period
P_{\rm mod}\sim
21
days. Sidepeaks with similar separations are
seen, corresponding to
P_{\rm mod}
ranging from 20 d to 25 d.

EPIC 251248825
(C11) =
OGLE-BLG-RRLYR-17931

EPIC 251248826
(C11) =
OGLE-BLG-RRLYR-18439
- In the
Petersen diagram this cRRd star lies among the “OGLE-clump” of short-period
stars identified by that survey (Soszynski
et al.
2014b).

EPIC 251248827
(C11) =
OGLE-BLG-RRLYR-18912
- This
star sits on the classical RRd curve, with approximately equal power in the
fundamental and first-overtone modes.

EPIC 251248828
(C11, aRRd) =
OGLE-BLG-RRLYR-24137
. This
‘anomalous’ RRd star was studied in detail by Smolec
et al.
(2015b) and
characterized there as an “intriguing triple-mode RR Lyrae star with period
doubling". In the Petersen diagram it lies significantly below the ‘classical’
RRd curve but not so low as to be among the ‘peculiar’ RRd stars. The fundamental
mode dominates over the first-overtone mode, as is typical for aRRd stars.

EPIC 251248830
(C11) - This faint (20
th
magnitude)
star was not observed by the
OGLE
survey thus the periods and
amplitudes are based on the
K2
photometry only. Its RRd nature is well
established. The periods are short and typical for a metal-rich cRRd star.
The amplitudes of the two modes are similar with the fundamental mode slightly
larger than that of the first-overtone.

EPIC 251456808
(C12) - At Kp=20.31 this star is one of the
faintest in the
K2
RRd-star sample. The CSS photometry (144
measurements) established that
<
V
>
=20.08
\pm
0.04 mag but are too
uncertain for reliable period determinations.

EPIC 251521080
(C17) - The
K2
photometry appears to be
contaminated by the light from a reddish star of comparable brightness
(
\sim
18th mag) located
\sim
5 arcsec to the north. Similar contamination is
seen in the Catalina CSS data but does not seem to have much affected the
Catalina MLS measurements.

EPIC 251629085
(C17) - In the Petersen diagram this star
lies on the curve occupied by ‘classical’ RRd stars, on the short period side,
with
P_{1}
=0.344182 d. The very large amplitude ratio,

A_{1}
/
A_{0}
=6.36
\pm
0.18, is caused primarily by the low
A_{0}=25.9\pm 0.6
, thus the light
curve shows little amplitude variation. See Table 7 for
SDSS/SEGUE
physical
characteristics, where the measured spectroscopic
[Fe/H]=–1.40
\pm
0.07 agrees extremely well with the metal abundance derived
from the
K2
photometry (Table 8).

EPIC 251809825
(C17) - Both radial pulsation modes are non-stationary.

EPIC 251809832
(C17) - This star is faint, with Kp=19.5 mag. An additional
significant signal, possibly due to light contamination, is seen at
P
=0.07665 d
(amplitude 4.6 mmag, period ratio relative to
P_{1}
of 0.1977).

EPIC 251809860
(C17) - The
K2
photometry of this faint star
is sufficient to identify it as a cRRd star.

EPIC 251809870
(C17) - Very faint, with Kp = 20.45
mag.

TV Libræ
=
Gaia
6321161342439508480,
with period 0.2696 d and [Fe/H]=–0.53 dex, is classified ‘RRab’ in DR3 and
by Crestani et al. (2021b), but in Fig. 12a it appears to be located among the
metal-rich RRc stars. The RRab classification is consistent with its
asymmetric light curve and with the high amplitudes given by Crestani et al.
(
A_{V}
=1.241 mag) and by
Gaia
(
A_{G}
=0.85 mag), both of which are
considerably higher than the 0.55 mag upper limit that one usually sees for RRc
stars. Its extremely short period appears to be unique among RRab stars, and
its spectrum is unusual, showing overabundant [
\alpha
/Fe] ratios (Liu et al.
2013). TV Lib almost certainly is an unusual short-period RRab star.

HY Comæ Berenices
=
Gaia

3946316423735761536, with period 0.4486 d and [Fe/H] =
-1.75\pm 0.02
dex
(Crestani et al. 2021a,b) was classified ‘RRc’ by Crestani, DR2 and DR3.
Consistent with this are the sinusoidal light curves seen in Fig.4 of Soper et
al. (2022), and its location among the RRc stars in the period-
A_{V}
diagram
constructed from the Crestani data. However, in Fig. 12a it is located among
the RRab stars. For an RRc star its period is unusually long.

SSS J205126.3-413741
=
Gaia

6677541412082758144, has period 0.406994 d, [Fe/H] =
-2.39\pm 0.11
dex, and
was classified ‘RRc’ by Crestani et al. (2021a,b). However, in the
Gaia

DR2 catalogue it was classified ‘RRd’ (with
P_{1}=0.40699
day,

P_{0}=0.54652
day and
P_{1}/P_{0}
=0.7447). In the DR3 catalogue it was
classified ‘RRab’ (with
P_{0}=0.40699
day) but its low amplitude (
A_{V}
=0.36
mag) is not typical for short-period RRab stars. We conclude that this probably
is an RRd star, in accord with the DR2 classification.

RW Trianguli Australis
=
Gaia

5815008831122635520, with
P_{0}
=0.37404 d, [Fe/H]
=+0.12\pm 0.04
dex, and
relatively high amplitude (
A_{V}
=0.72 mag), was classified ‘RRab’ by Crestani
et al. (2021a,b) and by the
GEOS
RR Lyr database (Le Borgne et al. 2007,2012).
However the
Gaia
DR2 and DR3 catalogues, despite finding the same period,
classify it as ‘RRc’. Given its relatively high amplitude and location among
the short-period metal-rich RRab stars in Fig. 12a, the ‘RRab’ classification
is favoured.

V5644 Sagittarii
=
Gaia

6736741901293501056 (DR3) = ASAS 183952-3200.9, is, according to Crestani et
al. (2021a,b), an ‘RRd’ star with metal abundance [Fe/H] =

-1.69\pm 0.01
dex and period 0.3424736 d (only one of the two periods is
given). It is not in the
Gaia
DR2 catalogue (and not in the
GEOS
RR Lyr
database) but the DR3 and ASAS catalogues both give
P_{0}
\sim
0.46125 d and
type ‘RRab’. If both detected periods are present then V5644 Sgr may be
doubly-periodic with
P_{1}/P_{0}
=0.7425. In Fig.12a its location is close to the
expected location for an RRd star.

CN Lyræ
=
Gaia
4539434124372063744,
with period
0.41138
d, amplitude
A_{V}=0.50
mag, [Fe/H]
spec
=
-0.08\pm 0.05
dex, and location in the period-
A_{V}
diagram amongst
the long-period RRc stars, is classified ‘RRc’ by Crestani et al. (2021a,b).
However, in the
Gaia
DR2 and DR3 catalogues, and in the
GEOS
RR Lyr
database, CN Lyr is classified ‘RRab’, which is consistent with its location
among the metal-rich RRab stars in Fig. 12a.

FW Lupi
=
Gaia
6005656897473385600, with
period
0.48417
d, amplitude
A_{V}
=0.39 mag, and [Fe/H]
spec
=

-0.17\pm 0.02
dex, is classified ‘RRab’ by Crestani et al. (2021a,b) and by
both
Gaia
catalogues. Such a classification is also consistent with its
location among the metal-rich RRab stars in Fig. 12a. However, in the
period-amplitude diagram its
A_{V}
is very low for a (non-Blazhko) short-period
RRab star, which brings this classification into question.
